# List of Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy launches

List of Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy launches

For the most recent Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy launch, please refer to [F9-701](#F9-700) and [FH-14](#FH-014) respectively.

| [List of Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy launches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Falcon_9_and_Falcon_Heavy_launches) |
| --- |
| [2010–2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Falcon_9_and_Falcon_Heavy_launches_(2010%E2%80%932019) "List of Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy launches (2010–2019)") · [2020–2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Falcon_9_and_Falcon_Heavy_launches_(2020%E2%80%932022) "List of Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy launches (2020–2022)") · [2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Falcon_9_and_Falcon_Heavy_launches_(2023) "List of Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy launches (2023)") · [2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Falcon_9_and_Falcon_Heavy_launches_(2024) "List of Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy launches (2024)") · [2025](#2025) · [2026](#2026) · [future](#Future_launches) |

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/72/F9_and_Heavy_visu.png/500px-F9_and_Heavy_visu.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:F9_and_Heavy_visu.png) Left to right: [Falcon 9 v1.0](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_v1.0 "Falcon 9 v1.0"), [v1.1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_v1.1 "Falcon 9 v1.1"), [v1.2 "Full Thrust"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_Full_Thrust "Falcon 9 Full Thrust"), [Falcon 9 Block 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_Block_5 "Falcon 9 Block 5"), [Falcon Heavy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_Heavy "Falcon Heavy"), and Falcon Heavy Block 5.

As of November 7, 2026, rockets from the [Falcon 9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9 "Falcon 9") family have been launched 715 times, with 712 full mission successes, two mission failures during launch,[&#8202;](#ref-back-1-0)[\[a\]](#refnote-1) one mission failure before launch,[&#8202;](#ref-back-2-0)[\[b\]](#refnote-2) and one partial failure.[&#8202;](#ref-back-3-0)[\[c\]](#refnote-3)

Designed and operated by [SpaceX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX "SpaceX"), the Falcon 9 family includes the retired versions [Falcon 9 v1.0](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_v1.0 "Falcon 9 v1.0"), launched five times from June 2010 to March 2013; [Falcon 9 v1.1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_v1.1 "Falcon 9 v1.1"), launched 15 times from September 2013 to January 2016; and [Falcon 9 v1.2 "Full Thrust"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_Full_Thrust "Falcon 9 Full Thrust") (blocks 3 and 4), launched 36 times from December 2015 to June 2018. The active "Full Thrust" variant [Falcon 9 Block 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_Block_5 "Falcon 9 Block 5") has launched 645 times since May 2018. [Falcon Heavy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_Heavy "Falcon Heavy"), a [heavy-lift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heavy-lift_launch_vehicle "Heavy-lift launch vehicle") derivative of Falcon 9, combining a strengthened central core with two Falcon 9 first stages as side boosters[&#8202;](#ref-back-sxf9o20100508-0)[\[1\]](#refnote-sxf9o20100508) has launched 14 times since February 2018.

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5e/Falcon_9_First_Stage_Booster.jpg/500px-Falcon_9_First_Stage_Booster.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Falcon_9_First_Stage_Booster.jpg) Falcon 9 at [Dish Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dish_Network "Dish Network")'s [Littleton, Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Littleton,_Colorado "Littleton, Colorado") office.

The Falcon design features [reusable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reusable_launch_system "Reusable launch system") first-stage boosters, which land either on a ground pad near the launch site or on a [drone ship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_spaceport_drone_ship "Autonomous spaceport drone ship") at sea.[&#8202;](#ref-back-pm20120207-0)[\[2\]](#refnote-pm20120207) In December 2015, Falcon 9 became the first rocket to [land propulsively](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VTVL "VTVL") after delivering a payload into orbit.[&#8202;](#ref-back-6-0)[\[3\]](#refnote-6) This reusability results in significantly reduced [launch costs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_launch_market_competition "Space launch market competition"), as the cost of the first stage constitutes the majority of the cost of a new rocket.[&#8202;](#ref-back-7-0)[\[4\]](#refnote-7)[&#8202;](#ref-back-8-0)[\[5\]](#refnote-8) [Falcon family boosters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Falcon_9_first-stage_boosters "List of Falcon 9 first-stage boosters") have successfully landed 674 times in 687 attempts. A total of 56 boosters have flown multiple missions, with a record of 37 missions by a booster, [B1067](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Falcon_9_first-stage_boosters#B1067 "List of Falcon 9 first-stage boosters"). SpaceX has also reflown fairing halves more than 300 times, with SN168 (40 times; the most reflown rocket stage to space)[&#8202;](#ref-back-9-0)[\[6\]](#refnote-9) and SN155 (40 times) being the most reflown passive and active fairing halves respectively.[&#8202;](#ref-back-10-0)[\[7\]](#refnote-10)

Typical missions include launches of SpaceX's [Starlink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starlink "Starlink") satellites (accounting for a majority of the Falcon manifest since January 2020), [Dragon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Dragon_2 "SpaceX Dragon 2") crew and cargo missions to the [International Space Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Space_Station "International Space Station"), and launches of commercial and military satellites to LEO, polar, and geosynchronous orbits. The heaviest payload launched on Falcon is a batch of 24 [Starlink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starlink "Starlink") V2-Mini satellites weighing about 17,500kg (38,600lb) total, first flown in February 2024,[&#8202;](#ref-back-s6-0)[\[8\]](#refnote-s6) landing on [JRTI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_spaceport_drone_ship "Autonomous spaceport drone ship"). The heaviest payload launched to [geostationary transfer orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geostationary_transfer_orbit "Geostationary transfer orbit") (GTO) was the 9,200kg (20,300lb) [Jupiter-3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EchoStar#Satellite_fleet "EchoStar") on July 29, 2023. Launches to higher orbits have included [DSCOVR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deep_Space_Climate_Observatory "Deep Space Climate Observatory") and [IMAP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstellar_Mapping_and_Acceleration_Probe "Interstellar Mapping and Acceleration Probe") to [Sun–Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth's_orbit "Earth's orbit") [Lagrange point L1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagrange_1_point "Lagrange 1 point"), [TESS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transiting_Exoplanet_Survey_Satellite "Transiting Exoplanet Survey Satellite") to a lunar flyby, a [Tesla Roadster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elon_Musk's_Tesla_Roadster "Elon Musk's Tesla Roadster") [demonstration payload](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_Heavy_test_flight "Falcon Heavy test flight") to a [heliocentric orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heliocentric_orbit "Heliocentric orbit") extending past the [orbit of Mars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbit_of_Mars "Orbit of Mars"), DART and [Hera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hera_(space_mission) "Hera (space mission)") to the asteroid [Didymos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/65803_Didymos "65803 Didymos"), Euclid to [Sun-Earth Lagrange point L2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagrange_point "Lagrange point"), [Psyche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psyche_(spacecraft) "Psyche (spacecraft)") to the asteroid [16 Psyche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/16_Psyche "16 Psyche"), and [Europa Clipper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europa_Clipper "Europa Clipper") to [Europa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europa_(moon) "Europa (moon)") (a moon of [Jupiter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jupiter "Jupiter")).

## Launch statistics

Rockets from the Falcon 9 family have a success rate of 99.58% and have been launched 715 times over 16years, resulting in 712 full successes, two in-flight failures ( [SpaceX CRS-7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_CRS-7 "SpaceX CRS-7") and [Starlink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starlink "Starlink") Group 9–3), one pre-flight failure ( [AMOS-6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AMOS-6_(satellite) "AMOS-6 (satellite)") while being prepared for an on-pad [static fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Static_fire "Static fire") test), and one partial failure ( [SpaceX CRS-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_CRS-1 "SpaceX CRS-1"), which delivered its cargo to the [International Space Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Space_Station "International Space Station") (ISS), but a [secondary payload](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secondary_payload "Secondary payload") was stranded in a lower-than-planned orbit). The active version of the rocket, the [Falcon 9 Block 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_Block_5 "Falcon 9 Block 5"), has flown 644 times successfully and failed once (Starlink Group 9–3), resulting in a 99.84% success rate.

In 2022, the Falcon 9 set a new record with 60 successful launches by the same launch vehicle type in a calendar year. This surpassed the previous record held by [Soyuz-U](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soyuz-U "Soyuz-U"), which had 47 launches (45 successful) in 1979.[&#8202;](#ref-back-EM201022-0)[\[9\]](#refnote-EM201022)
In 2023, the Falcon family of rockets (including the Falcon Heavy) had 96 successful launches, surpassing the [63 launches (61 successful)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_R-7_launches_(1980%E2%80%931984)#1980 "List of R-7 launches (1980–1984)") of the [R-7 rocket family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R-7_(rocket_family) "R-7 (rocket family)") in 1980.[&#8202;](#ref-back-13-0)[\[d\]](#refnote-13)[&#8202;](#ref-back-14-0)[\[10\]](#refnote-14)
In 2024, SpaceX broke their own record with 134 total Falcon flights (133 successful) accounting for over half of all [orbital launches that year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_in_spaceflight "2024 in spaceflight").
In 2025, SpaceX broke their own record again with 165 total Falcon flights (165 successful).

The Falcon 9 has evolved through several versions: [v1.0](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_v1.0 "Falcon 9 v1.0") was launched five times from 2010 to 2013, [v1.1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_v1.1 "Falcon 9 v1.1") launched 15 times from 2013 to 2016, [Full Thrust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_Full_Thrust "Falcon 9 Full Thrust") launched 36 times from 2015 to 2018. The most recent version, Block 5, was introduced in May 2018.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:4-0)[\[11\]](#refnote-:4) With each iteration, the Falcon 9 boosters have become more powerful and capable of vertical landing, while fairings simultaneously performing water landing, before being scouped out of water. As vertical landings and fairing recovery operations became more commonplace, SpaceX focused on streamlining the refurbishment process for boosters and fairings, making it faster and more cost-effective.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nsf20180517-0)[\[12\]](#refnote-nsf20180517)

The [Falcon Heavy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_Heavy "Falcon Heavy") derivative is a heavy-lift launch vehicle composed of three Falcon 9 first-stage boosters. The central core is reinforced, while the side boosters feature aerodynamic [nosecone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nosecone "Nosecone") instead of the usual [interstage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstage "Interstage").[&#8202;](#ref-back-17-0)[\[13\]](#refnote-17)

Falcon 9 first-stage boosters landed successfully in 674 of 687 attempts (98.11%), with 649 out of 655 (99.08%) for the Falcon 9 Block 5 version. A total of 647 [re-flights of first stage boosters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Falcon_9_first-stage_boosters "List of Falcon 9 first-stage boosters") have all successfully launched their second stages and, all but one, their payloads.

## Past launches

For just Falcon Heavy, see [Falcon Heavy §Launches and payloads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_Heavy#Launches_and_payloads "Falcon Heavy").

### 2010 to 2019

For launches prior to 2020, please refer to [List of Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy launches (2010–2019)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Falcon_9_and_Falcon_Heavy_launches_(2010%E2%80%932019) "List of Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy launches (2010–2019)").

From June 2010, to the end of 2019, [Falcon 9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9 "Falcon 9") was launched 77 times, with 75 full mission successes, one partial failure and one total loss of the spacecraft. In addition, one rocket and its payload were destroyed on the launch pad during the fueling process before a static fire test was set to occur. [Falcon Heavy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_Heavy "Falcon Heavy") was launched three times, all successful.

The first Falcon 9 version, [Falcon 9 v1.0](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_v1.0 "Falcon 9 v1.0"), was launched five times from June 2010, to March 2013, its successor [Falcon 9 v1.1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_v1.1 "Falcon 9 v1.1") 15 times from September 2013, to January 2016, and the [Falcon 9 Full Thrust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_Full_Thrust "Falcon 9 Full Thrust") (through Block 4) 36 times from December 2015, to June 2018. The latest Full Thrust variant, [Block 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_Block_5 "Falcon 9 Block 5"), was introduced in May 2018,[&#8202;](#ref-back-:4-1)[\[11\]](#refnote-:4) and launched 21 times before the end of 2019.

### 2020 to 2022

For launches between 2020 and 2022, please refer to [List of Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy launches (2020–2022)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Falcon_9_and_Falcon_Heavy_launches_(2020%E2%80%932022) "List of Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy launches (2020–2022)").

From January 2020, to the end of 2022, [Falcon 9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9 "Falcon 9") was launched 117 times, all successful, and landed boosters successfully on 111 of 114 attempts. [Falcon Heavy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_Heavy "Falcon Heavy") was launched once and was successful, including landing of the mission's two side boosters.

### 2023

For launches in 2023, please refer to [List of Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy launches (2023)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Falcon_9_and_Falcon_Heavy_launches_(2023) "List of Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy launches (2023)").

SpaceX conducted 96 Falcon family vehicle launches (91 Falcon 9 and 5 Falcon Heavy) in 2023. It surpassed both the company's own single-year launch record of 61 and the global annual record of 64 launches, coming close to its previously announced goal of 100 Falcon launches in the year.[&#8202;](#ref-back-21-0)[\[14\]](#refnote-21)[&#8202;](#ref-back-22-0)[\[15\]](#refnote-22)[&#8202;](#ref-back-note100-0)[\[h\]](#refnote-note100)

The company's payload delivery capacity also rose, with approximately 1,200 tonnes (2,600,000lb) sent to orbit.[&#8202;](#ref-back-24-0)[\[16\]](#refnote-24)

### 2024

For launches in 2024, please refer to [List of Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy launches (2024)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Falcon_9_and_Falcon_Heavy_launches_(2024) "List of Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy launches (2024)").

SpaceX conducted 134 Falcon family vehicle launches (132 Falcon 9 and 2 Falcon Heavy) in 2024, including the failed [Starlink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starlink "Starlink") Group 9-3 mission.[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto-0)[\[17\]](#refnote-auto) It again broke the global single-year launch record of 98 launches in a year (set by SpaceX in the previous year with 96 Falcon and 2 [Starship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starship "SpaceX Starship") launches).

The company had set initial launch targets for the year of approximately 144 launches, or an average of 12 per month, accounting for potential delays due to weather, technical issues, and scheduled maintenance.[&#8202;](#ref-back-26-0)[\[18\]](#refnote-26)[&#8202;](#ref-back-27-0)[\[19\]](#refnote-27) However, subsequent statements from SpaceX leadership indicated a potential increase to 148 launches, an average of 13 launches per month.[&#8202;](#ref-back-28-0)[\[20\]](#refnote-28)[&#8202;](#ref-back-29-0)[\[21\]](#refnote-29) Later in November 2024, due to launch or recovery failures leading to several mishap investigations and delays, SpaceX leadership lowered the year's launch projections to approximately 136 launches in the year, which was subsequently missed by two launches.[&#8202;](#ref-back-30-0)[\[22\]](#refnote-30)

The company's payload delivery capacity also rose, with more than 1,498 tonnes (3,303,000lb) (only 85.5% of the launches were reported launch masses) sent to orbit.[&#8202;](#ref-back-31-0)[\[23\]](#refnote-31)

### 2025

SpaceX conducted 165 Falcon 9 launches in 2025, a new annual launch record, and sending an estimated 2,400 tonnes (5,300,000lb) to orbit.[&#8202;](#ref-back-32-0)[\[24\]](#refnote-32)

In November 2024, the company outlined ambitious launch targets for the year, with initial projections of more than 150 launches, or an average of 12 to 13 per month, accounting for potential delays due to weather, technical issues, and scheduled maintenance.[&#8202;](#ref-back-33-0)[\[25\]](#refnote-33) Later, in December, SpaceX President Gwynne Shotwell stated they were expecting 175 to 180 launches in 2025, or an average of 14 to 15 per month.[&#8202;](#ref-back-34-0)[\[26\]](#refnote-34)[&#8202;](#ref-back-35-0)[\[27\]](#refnote-35) Later they reduced the target to 170 launches in the year and further to 165 launches in the year or an average of 13 to 14 launches per month.[&#8202;](#ref-back-36-0)[\[28\]](#refnote-36)

| Both Blue Ghost Mission 1[&#8202;](#ref-back-50-0)[\[40\]](#refnote-50)[&#8202;](#ref-back-51-0)[\[41\]](#refnote-51) and Hakuto-R Mission 2 (Resilience lander)[&#8202;](#ref-back-52-0)[\[42\]](#refnote-52)[&#8202;](#ref-back-ispr25122-0)[\[43\]](#refnote-ispr25122) launched together on a single rocket (first of its kind deep space lander launch). Blue Ghost will carry 10 payloads for NASA's [Commercial Lunar Payload Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commercial_Lunar_Payload_Services "Commercial Lunar Payload Services") task order 19D mission and other separately contracted payloads.[&#8202;](#ref-back-54-0)[\[44\]](#refnote-54) Resilience is the second lunar lander built by Japanese company [ispace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ispace_(Japanese_company) "Ispace (Japanese company)") and will deliver TENACIOUS mini rover to the lunar surface. To house both landers in the payload fairing, SpaceX used a new dual-launch carrying structure. The upper stage [impacted the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_SpaceX_lunar_impact "2026 SpaceX lunar impact") on August 5, 2026.[&#8202;](#ref-back-55-0)[\[45\]](#refnote-55) |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

| Launch of 22 Starlink v2mini satellites to a 535km (332mi) orbit at an inclination of 53° to expand internet constellation. Even though the second stage was expected to deorbit for a controlled splashdown, the second stage's deorbit burn did not occur, causing it to remain in orbit. This was the third time in six months that a second stage had encountered a problem in flight.[&#8202;](#ref-back-65-0)[\[55\]](#refnote-65) The stage made its uncontrolled reentry over Poland between 04:46 and 04:48, local time, on February 19.[&#8202;](#ref-back-66-0)[\[56\]](#refnote-66) Several tanks that crashed in the western part of the country were collected by the [Polish police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_police "Polish police").[&#8202;](#ref-back-BBCPoland-0)[\[57\]](#refnote-BBCPoland) |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

| Launch of 21 Starlink v2mini satellites, including 13 with direct-to-cell connectivity, to a 559km (347mi) orbit at an inclination of 43° to expand internet constellation. A fuel leak started in one of the nine Merlin engines in the first stage 85 seconds after liftoff. However, because of the altitude of the rocket, there was no oxygen to ignite the fuel, allowing the first stage to completed its ascent without issue. However, 45 seconds after the booster landed, enough oxygen had entered the engine compartment where the leak occurred, creating a large fire. The fire resulted in the structural failure of one of the landing legs, leading to the booster tipping over and being destroyed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-85-0)[\[75\]](#refnote-85) SpaceX voluntarily paused launches for more than a week as it investigated the issue.[&#8202;](#ref-back-86-0)[\[76\]](#refnote-86) |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

Based on the number of gaps in the catalog it appears that this launch only deployed 11 payloads, likely indicating a larger Starshield version. This mission marks the shortest turnaround time for any Falcon booster at 9 days, 3 hours, 39 minutes and 28 seconds. |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

| SmallSat rideshare mission to a 550–600km (340–370mi) orbit at an inclination of 45°. Launch featured a rare Falcon 9 landing at LZ-2, as the booster from the CRS-32 launch just hours before was still on LZ-1. 425Sat-3 is the third [synthetic-aperture radar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synthetic-aperture_radar "Synthetic-aperture radar") satellite for the constellation and the fourth flight of Korea 425 Project for the South Korean military. Also on the flight are Tomorrow.io's Tomorrow-S7 satellite and Atmos Space Cargo's PHOENIX re-entry capsule. This mission marks the 300th launch from SLC-40. |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

| Long-duration mission. Will ferry four [Expedition 73](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expedition_73 "Expedition 73") / [74](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expedition_74 "Expedition 74") crew members to the ISS. Endeavour is the first Dragon to fly beyond the initial certification of 5 flights per Dragon. During a press conference before Crew-11, Gerstenmaier said the landing of B1094 will be the final use of [Landing Zone 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Landing_Zone_1 "SpaceX Landing Zone 1"), but they will continue to use [Landing Zone 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Landing_Zone_2 "SpaceX Landing Zone 2"). That site, [Launch Complex 13](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Canaveral_Launch_Complex_13 "Cape Canaveral Launch Complex 13"), is being transitioned back to a launch pad to be jointly used by [Phantom Space Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phantom_Space_Corporation "Phantom Space Corporation") and [Vaya Space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaya_Space "Vaya Space"), while SpaceX will make new landing pads within the vicinity of [LC‑39A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kennedy_Space_Center_Launch_Complex_39A "Kennedy Space Center Launch Complex 39A") and [SLC‑40](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Canaveral_Space_Launch_Complex_40 "Cape Canaveral Space Launch Complex 40").[&#8202;](#ref-back-172-0)[\[161\]](#refnote-172) Fastest Crew Dragon rendezvous to date. |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

| Launch of 28 Starlink v2mini optimized satellites to a 279km (173mi) orbit at an inclination of 53° to expand internet constellation. | In September 2020, NASA selected SpaceX to launch the IMAP mission, which will help researchers better understand the boundary of the heliosphere, a magnetic barrier surrounding our solar system. ### 2026

As of November 7, SpaceX has conducted122 Falcon family vehicle launches (119 Falcon 9 and 3 Falcon Heavy) in 2026. SpaceX President Gwynne Shotwell stated in _Time_ magazine they are expecting "maybe 140, 145-ish" Falcon 9 launches in 2026.[&#8202;](#ref-back-263-0)[\[252\]](#refnote-263)

| Ka-band satellite for the APAC region. Originally planned to launch on a [Ariane 6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ariane_6 "Ariane 6"), payload was shifted to Falcon Heavy following Ariane development delays. B1075 had previously flown 21 Falcon 9 missions from Vandenberg before being converted into a side booster for this launch. It landed at [LZ-40](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Landing_Zone_40 "SpaceX Landing Zone 40"), becoming the first Falcon Heavy side booster to touch down at the pad. This mission was the eighth Falcon-family launch to use a second stage equipped with the medium mission-extension kit, required for a long coast phase between second-stage ignitions. |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

| Maiden launch of Starfall as Starfall Demo. Starfall is SpaceX, mass-produced reentry vehicle designed to autonomously transport valuable payloads safely back from space to Earth, including for in-orbit manufacturing. Starfall is a cylindrical shaped capsule approximately 0.75 meters tall with a diameter of 3.1 meters, weighing approximately 2,100 kilograms, and capable of carrying 1,000 kilogram of payload. This mission was the ninth Falcon-family launch to use a second stage equipped with the medium mission-extension kit, required for a long coast phase between second-stage ignitions. |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

| Classified mission for the United States Space Force, suspected to be part of the new Starshield-based Space Data Network Backbone. It is possibly a batch of classified LEO satellites for a US government entity based on SpaceX's Starshield satellite bus. SpaceX set a new record for the shortest time between two Falcon launches at 38 minutes. | Launch is part of Phase 3 Lane 2 US Space Force contract awarded in 2025. First NRO launch on a Falcon Heavy. Fastest turnaround for a Falcon Heavy Launch. First time all four SpaceX ground landing zones (LZ-1, LZ-2, LZ-4 and LZ-40) hosted Falcon booster landings within 24 hours. Although LZ-1 stays retired, it was temporarily reactivated to assist booster landings for this mission, as LZ-40 was already occupied by the booster used during SpaceX's [Crew-13](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Crew-13 "SpaceX Crew-13") mission.[&#8202;](#ref-back-389-0)[\[378\]](#refnote-389) This mission was the tenth Falcon-family launch to use a second stage equipped with the medium mission-extension kit, required for a long coast phase between second-stage ignitions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-390-0)[\[379\]](#refnote-390) |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

## Future launches

Future launches are listed chronologically when firm plans are in place. The order of the later launches is much less certain. Tentative launch dates and mission details are sourced from multiple locations.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nextSFupcoming-0)[\[390\]](#refnote-nextSFupcoming)[&#8202;](#ref-back-sfn_ls-0)[\[391\]](#refnote-sfn_ls)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FAACOPA-0)[\[392\]](#refnote-FAACOPA)[&#8202;](#ref-back-cooper-0)[\[393\]](#refnote-cooper) Launches are expected to take place "no earlier than" (NET) the listed date.

| Astrobotic's [Griffin lunar lander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Griffin_(spacecraft) "Griffin (spacecraft)") was originally expected to deliver NASA's _[VIPER](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VIPER_(rover) "VIPER (rover)")_ rover to the [lunar south pole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_south_pole "Lunar south pole") prior to its cancellation. Booster recovery method is unconfirmed, but could possibly feature the first Falcon Heavy center core recovery attempt since [STP-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STP-2 "STP-2").[&#8202;](#ref-back-411-0)[\[400\]](#refnote-411) Amidst cost growth and delays to readiness of the rover and the Griffin lander, the VIPER program was ended in July 2024, with the rover planned to be disassembled and its instruments and components reused for other lunar missions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nasa-1)[\[401\]](#refnote-nasa)[&#8202;](#ref-back-413-0)[\[402\]](#refnote-413)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Boyle_2024-0)[\[403\]](#refnote-Boyle_2024) NASA expects the primary objectives of VIPER to be fulfilled by an array of other missions planned for the next several years.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Boyle_2024-1)[\[403\]](#refnote-Boyle_2024) NASA indicated it would continue to support the Griffin lander to go to the moon even if this meant a mass simulator in place of the VIPER rover.[&#8202;](#ref-back-415-0)[\[404\]](#refnote-415) Astrolab's FLIP rover is to replace the VIPER rover.[&#8202;](#ref-back-416-0)[\[405\]](#refnote-416) |  |  |  |  |  |

## Notable launches

### First flights and contracts

Main articles: [Dragon Spacecraft Qualification Unit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dragon_Spacecraft_Qualification_Unit "Dragon Spacecraft Qualification Unit"), [COTS Demo Flight 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COTS_Demo_Flight_1 "COTS Demo Flight 1"), [Dragon C2+](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dragon_C2+ "Dragon C2+"), and [SpaceX CRS-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_CRS-1 "SpaceX CRS-1")

Launch of Falcon 9 Flight 1 with a [boilerplate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boilerplate_(spaceflight) "Boilerplate (spaceflight)") Dragon[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b7/SpX_CRS-1_berthed_-_cropped.jpg/960px-SpX_CRS-1_berthed_-_cropped.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:SpX_CRS-1_berthed_-_cropped.jpg) [Dragon CRS-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dragon_CRS-1 "Dragon CRS-1") berthed to the [International Space Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Space_Station "International Space Station") (ISS) on October 14, 2012, photographed from the [Cupola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cupola_(ISS_module) "Cupola (ISS module)").

On June 4, 2010, the first Falcon 9 launch successfully placed a test payload into the intended orbit.[&#8202;](#ref-back-sfn20100604-0)[\[450\]](#refnote-sfn20100604) The second launch of Falcon 9 was [COTS Demo Flight 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COTS_Demo_Flight_1 "COTS Demo Flight 1"), which placed an operational [Dragon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Dragon "SpaceX Dragon") capsule in orbit on December 8, 2010.[&#8202;](#ref-back-BBCLaunchDec2010-0)[\[451\]](#refnote-BBCLaunchDec2010) The capsule re-entered the atmosphere after two orbits, allowing for testing the reentry procedures. The capsule was recovered off the coast of Mexico[&#8202;](#ref-back-SFN_Status-0)[\[452\]](#refnote-SFN_Status) and then placed on display at SpaceX headquarters.[&#8202;](#ref-back-464-0)[\[453\]](#refnote-464) The remaining objectives of the [NASA COTS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commercial_Orbital_Transportation_Services "Commercial Orbital Transportation Services") qualification program were combined into a single [Dragon C2+](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dragon_C2+ "Dragon C2+") mission, on the condition that all milestones would be validated in space before [berthing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spacecraft_docking_and_berthing_mechanisms "Spacecraft docking and berthing mechanisms") Dragon to the ISS.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Space_News_2011-0)[\[454\]](#refnote-Space_News_2011-07) The Dragon capsule was propelled to orbit in May 2012, and following successful tests in the next days it was grabbed with [the station's robotic arm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mobile_Servicing_System "Mobile Servicing System") (Canadarm2) and docked to the ISS docking port for the first time on May 25. After successfully completing all the return procedures, the recovered Dragon C2+ capsule was put on display at [Kennedy Space Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kennedy_Space_Center "Kennedy Space Center").[&#8202;](#ref-back-466-0)[\[455\]](#refnote-466) Thus, Falcon 9 and Dragon became the first fully commercially developed launcher to deliver a payload to the International Space Station, paving the way for SpaceX and NASA to sign the first [Commercial Resupply Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commercial_Resupply_Services "Commercial Resupply Services") agreement for cargo deliveries.[&#8202;](#ref-back-certified-0)[\[456\]](#refnote-certified)

The first operational cargo resupply mission to ISS, the fourth flight of Falcon 9, was launched in October 2012. An engine suffered a loss of pressure at 76 seconds after liftoff, which caused an automatic shutdown of that engine, but the remaining eight first-stage engines continued to burn and the Dragon capsule reached orbit successfully and thus demonstrated the rocket's "engine out" capability in flight.[&#8202;](#ref-back-flight4engineanomaly-0)[\[457\]](#refnote-flight4engineanomaly) Due to ISS visiting vehicle safety rules, at NASA's request, the secondary payload Orbcomm-2 was released into a lower-than-intended orbit.[&#8202;](#ref-back-OrbcommTotalLoss-0)[\[458\]](#refnote-OrbcommTotalLoss) Despite this incident, Orbcomm said they gathered useful test data from the mission and later in 2014, launched more satellites via SpaceX.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nyt-0)[\[459\]](#refnote-nyt) The mission continued to rendezvous and berth the Dragon capsule with the ISS where the ISS crew unloaded its payload and reloaded the spacecraft with cargo for return to Earth.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Dragon_mission_report_CRS-0)[\[460\]](#refnote-Dragon_mission_report_CRS)

Following unsuccessful attempts at recovering the first stage with parachutes, SpaceX upgraded to a much larger first stage booster and with greater thrust, termed [Falcon 9 v1.1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_v1.1 "Falcon 9 v1.1"), and performed a demonstration flight of this version in September 2013.[&#8202;](#ref-back-sfn20130928-0)[\[461\]](#refnote-sfn20130928) After the [second stage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multistage_rocket "Multistage rocket") separation and delivering [CASSIOPE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CASSIOPE "CASSIOPE"), a very small payload relative to the rocket's capability, SpaceX conducted a novel [high-altitude, high-velocity flight test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_first-stage_landing_tests "Falcon 9 first-stage landing tests") wherein the booster attempted to reenter the lower atmosphere in a controlled manner and decelerate to a simulated over-water landing.[&#8202;](#ref-back-pa20130930-0)[\[462\]](#refnote-pa20130930)

### Loss of CRS-7 mission

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/02/SpaceX_CRS-7_launch_failure.jpg/960px-SpaceX_CRS-7_launch_failure.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:SpaceX_CRS-7_launch_failure.jpg) SpaceX CRS-7 disintegrating two minutes after liftoff, as seen from a NASA tracking camera.

Main article: [SpaceX CRS-7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_CRS-7 "SpaceX CRS-7")

In June 2015, [Falcon 9 Flight 19](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_Flight_19 "Falcon 9 Flight 19") carried a [Dragon capsule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dragon_capsule "Dragon capsule") on the seventh [Commercial Resupply Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commercial_Resupply_Services "Commercial Resupply Services") mission to the [ISS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Space_Station "International Space Station"). The second stage disintegrated due to an internal helium tank failure while the first stage was still burning normally. This was the first (and only as of May 2024) primary mission loss for any Falcon 9 rocket.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nyt-1)[\[463\]](#refnote-nyt) In addition to ISS consumables and experiments, this mission carried the first [International Docking Adapter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Docking_Adapter "International Docking Adapter") (IDA-1), whose loss delayed preparedness of the station's [US Orbital Segment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Orbital_Segment "US Orbital Segment") (USOS) for [future crewed missions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commercial_Crew_Development "Commercial Crew Development").[&#8202;](#ref-back-475-0)[\[464\]](#refnote-475)

Performance was nominal until T+140 seconds into launch when a cloud of white vapor appeared, followed by rapid loss of second-stage [LOX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LOX "LOX") tank pressure. The booster continued on its trajectory until complete vehicle breakup at T+150 seconds. The Dragon capsule was ejected from the disintegrating rocket and continued transmitting data until impact with the ocean. SpaceX officials stated that the capsule could have been recovered if the parachutes had deployed; however, the Dragon software did not include any provisions for parachute deployment in this situation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nsf-0)[\[465\]](#refnote-nsf) Subsequent investigations traced the cause of the accident to the failure of a strut that secured a helium bottle inside the second-stage LOX tank. With the helium pressurization system integrity breached, excess helium quickly flooded the tank, eventually causing it to burst from overpressure.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SpaceXJuly2015-0)[\[466\]](#refnote-SpaceXJuly2015)[&#8202;](#ref-back-478-0)[\[467\]](#refnote-478) NASA's independent accident investigation into the loss of SpaceX CRS-7 found that the failure of the strut which led to the breakup of the Falcon-9 represented a design error. Specifically, that industrial grade stainless steel had been used in a critical load path under cryogenic conditions and flight conditions, without additional part screening, and without regard to manufacturer recommendations.[&#8202;](#ref-back-479-0)[\[468\]](#refnote-479)

### Full-thrust version and first booster landings

Main articles: [Falcon 9 flight 20](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_flight_20 "Falcon 9 flight 20") and [SpaceX CRS-8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_CRS-8 "SpaceX CRS-8")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/ORBCOMM-2_First-Stage_Landing_%2823271687254%29.jpg/960px-ORBCOMM-2_First-Stage_Landing_%2823271687254%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:ORBCOMM-2_First-Stage_Landing_(23271687254).jpg) [Falcon 9 Flight 20](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_Flight_20 "Falcon 9 Flight 20") historic [first-stage landing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_reusable_launch_system_development_program "SpaceX reusable launch system development program") at [CCSFS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Canaveral_Air_Force_Station "Cape Canaveral Air Force Station") [Landing Zone 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landing_Zones_1_and_2 "Landing Zones 1 and 2"), December 22, 2015

After pausing launches for months, SpaceX launched on December 22, 2015, the highly anticipated return-to-flight mission after the loss of [CRS-7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CRS-7 "CRS-7"). This launch inaugurated a new [Falcon 9 Full Thrust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_Full_Thrust "Falcon 9 Full Thrust") version of its flagship rocket featuring increased performance, notably thanks to [subcooling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subcooling "Subcooling") of the propellants. After launching a constellation of 11 [Orbcomm-OG2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbcomm-OG2 "Orbcomm-OG2") second-generation satellites,[&#8202;](#ref-back-480-0)[\[469\]](#refnote-480) the first stage performed a [controlled-descent and landing test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_first-stage_landing_tests "Falcon 9 first-stage landing tests") for the eighth time, SpaceX attempted to land the booster on land for the first time. It managed to return the first stage successfully to the [Landing Zone 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landing_Zones_1_and_2 "Landing Zones 1 and 2") at [Cape Canaveral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Canaveral_Air_Force_Station "Cape Canaveral Air Force Station"), marking the first successful recovery of a rocket first stage that launched a payload to orbit.[&#8202;](#ref-back-481-0)[\[470\]](#refnote-481) After recovery, [the first stage booster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_booster_B1019 "Falcon 9 booster B1019") performed further ground tests and then was put on permanent display outside SpaceX's headquarters in [Hawthorne, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawthorne,_California "Hawthorne, California").[&#8202;](#ref-back-flight20-booster-0)[\[471\]](#refnote-flight20-booster)

On April 8, 2016, SpaceX delivered its [commercial resupply](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commercial_Resupply_Services "Commercial Resupply Services") mission to the International Space Station marking the return-to-flight of the Dragon capsule, after the loss of CRS-7. After separation, the [first-stage booster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_booster_B1021 "Falcon 9 booster B1021") slowed itself with a boostback maneuver, re-entered the atmosphere, executed an automated controlled descent and landed vertically onto the drone ship _[Of Course I Still Love You](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Of_Course_I_Still_Love_You "Of Course I Still Love You")_, marking the first successful landing of a rocket on a ship at sea.[&#8202;](#ref-back-natgeo-0)[\[472\]](#refnote-natgeo) This was the fourth attempt to land on a drone ship, as part of the company's experimental [controlled-descent and landing tests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_first-stage_landing_tests "Falcon 9 first-stage landing tests").[&#8202;](#ref-back-484-0)[\[473\]](#refnote-484)

### Loss of AMOS-6 on the launch pad

Main article: [AMOS-6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AMOS-6 "AMOS-6")

On September 1, 2016, the 29th Falcon 9 rocket exploded on the launchpad while propellant was being loaded for a routine pre-launch static fire test. The payload, Israeli satellite [AMOS-6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AMOS-6 "AMOS-6"), partly commissioned by [Facebook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facebook "Facebook"), was destroyed with the launcher.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Malik-0)[\[474\]](#refnote-Malik) On January 2, 2017, SpaceX released an official statement indicating that the cause of the failure was a buckled liner in several of the [COPV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COPV "COPV") tanks, causing perforations that allowed liquid and/or solid oxygen to accumulate underneath the COPVs carbon strands, which were subsequently ignited possibly due to friction of breaking strands.[&#8202;](#ref-back-486-0)[\[475\]](#refnote-486)

### Zuma launch

Main article: [Zuma (satellite)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zuma_(satellite) "Zuma (satellite)")

Zuma was a classified United States government satellite and was developed and built by [Northrop Grumman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northrop_Grumman "Northrop Grumman") at an estimated cost of US$3.5 billion.[&#8202;](#ref-back-markwatch-0)[\[476\]](#refnote-markwatch) Its launch, originally planned for mid-November 2017, was postponed to January 8, 2018, as [fairing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Payload_fairing "Payload fairing") tests for another SpaceX customer were assessed. Following a successful Falcon 9 launch, the first-stage booster landed at [LZ-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landing_Zones_1_and_2 "Landing Zones 1 and 2").[&#8202;](#ref-back-zuma-presskit2-0)[\[477\]](#refnote-zuma-presskit2) Unconfirmed reports suggested that the Zuma spacecraft was lost,[&#8202;](#ref-back-ZumaVerge2-0)[\[478\]](#refnote-ZumaVerge2) with claims that either the payload failed following orbital release, or that the customer-provided adapter failed to release the satellite from the upper stage, while other claims argued that Zuma was in orbit and operating covertly.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ZumaVerge2-1)[\[478\]](#refnote-ZumaVerge2) SpaceX's COO [Gwynne Shotwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwynne_Shotwell "Gwynne Shotwell") stated that their Falcon 9 "did everything correctly" and that "Information published that is contrary to this statement is categorically false".[&#8202;](#ref-back-ZumaVerge2-2)[\[478\]](#refnote-ZumaVerge2) A preliminary report indicated that the payload adapter, modified by Northrop Grumman after purchasing it from a subcontractor, failed to separate the satellite from the second stage under the zero gravity conditions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-490-0)[\[479\]](#refnote-490)[&#8202;](#ref-back-markwatch-1)[\[476\]](#refnote-markwatch) Due to the classified nature of the mission, no further official information is expected.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ZumaVerge2-3)[\[478\]](#refnote-ZumaVerge2)

### Falcon Heavy test flight

Main article: [Falcon Heavy test flight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_Heavy_test_flight "Falcon Heavy test flight")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Falcon_Heavy_Demo_Mission_%2840126461851%29.jpg/960px-Falcon_Heavy_Demo_Mission_%2840126461851%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Falcon_Heavy_Demo_Mission_(40126461851).jpg)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/52/Falcon_Heavy_Side_Boosters_landing_on_LZ1_and_LZ2_-_2018_%2825254688767%29.jpg/960px-Falcon_Heavy_Side_Boosters_landing_on_LZ1_and_LZ2_-_2018_%2825254688767%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Falcon_Heavy_Side_Boosters_landing_on_LZ1_and_LZ2_-_2018_(25254688767).jpg)

Liftoff of Falcon Heavy on its maiden flight (left) and its two side-boosters landing at [LZ-1 and LZ-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landing_Zones_1_and_2 "Landing Zones 1 and 2") a few minutes later (right)

The maiden launch of the [Falcon Heavy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_Heavy "Falcon Heavy") occurred on February6, 2018, making it the most powerful rocket since the [Saturn V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saturn_V "Saturn V"), with a theoretical payload capacity to [low Earth orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low_Earth_orbit "Low Earth orbit") more than double the [Delta IV Heavy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delta_IV_Heavy "Delta IV Heavy").[&#8202;](#ref-back-491-0)[\[480\]](#refnote-491)[&#8202;](#ref-back-NatGeo-2018-0)[\[481\]](#refnote-NatGeo-2018-02) Both side boosters landed nearly simultaneously after a ten-minute flight. The central core failed to land on a [floating platform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_spaceport_drone_ship "Autonomous spaceport drone ship") at sea.[&#8202;](#ref-back-middle-booster-0)[\[482\]](#refnote-middle-booster) The rocket carried [a car and a mannequin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elon_Musk's_Tesla_Roadster "Elon Musk's Tesla Roadster") to an [eccentric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_eccentricity "Orbital eccentricity") [heliocentric orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heliocentric_orbit "Heliocentric orbit") that reaches further than [aphelion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perihelion_and_aphelion "Perihelion and aphelion") of [Mars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mars "Mars").[&#8202;](#ref-back-PopMech-2018-0)[\[483\]](#refnote-PopMech-2018-02)

### First crewed flights

Main articles: [Crew Dragon Demo-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crew_Dragon_Demo-1 "Crew Dragon Demo-1") and [Crew Dragon Demo-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crew_Dragon_Demo-2 "Crew Dragon Demo-2")

On March 2, 2019, SpaceX launched its first orbital flight of [Dragon2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Dragon_2 "SpaceX Dragon 2") (Crew Dragon). It was an uncrewed mission to the [International Space Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Space_Station "International Space Station"). The Dragon contained a mannequin named Ripley, which was equipped with multiple sensors to gather data about how a human would feel during the flight. Along with the mannequin was 300 pounds of cargo of food and other supplies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-495-0)[\[484\]](#refnote-495) Also on board was Earth plush toy referred to as a "Super high tech zero-g indicator".[&#8202;](#ref-back-496-0)[\[485\]](#refnote-496) The toy became a hit with astronaut [Anne McClain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anne_McClain "Anne McClain"), who showed the plushy on the ISS each day[&#8202;](#ref-back-497-0)[\[486\]](#refnote-497) and also deciding to keep it on board to experience the crewed [SpX-DM2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpX-DM2 "SpX-DM2").

The Dragon spent six days in space, including five days docked to the International Space Station. During the time, various systems were tested to make sure the vehicle was ready for US astronauts [Doug Hurley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doug_Hurley "Doug Hurley") and [Bob Behnken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Behnken "Bob Behnken") to fly in it in 2020. The Dragon undocked and performed a re-entry burn before splashing down on March8, 2019, at 08:45EST, 320km (200mi) off the coast of Florida.[&#8202;](#ref-back-498-0)[\[487\]](#refnote-498)

SpaceX held a successful launch of the first commercial orbital human space flight on May30, 2020, crewed with NASA astronauts [Doug Hurley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doug_Hurley "Doug Hurley") and [Bob Behnken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Behnken "Bob Behnken"). Both astronauts focused on conducting tests on the Crew Dragon capsule. Crew Dragon successfully returned to Earth, splashing down in the Gulf of Mexico on August2, 2020.[&#8202;](#ref-back-499-0)[\[488\]](#refnote-499)

### Starlink 9-3 upper stage anomaly

On July 12, 2024, SpaceX launched a group of [Starlink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starlink "Starlink") satellites from [Vandenberg Space Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vandenberg_Space_Force_Base "Vandenberg Space Force Base") in California. While the booster performed nominally, including a successful droneship landing, the upper stage failed to relight for a second burn, with ice appearing to accumulate around the engine during the first burn due to a [liquid oxygen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liquid_oxygen "Liquid oxygen") leak that developed from [vibrational fatigue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vibration_fatigue "Vibration fatigue") which led to a crack in a pressure sensor line.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SpaceX_Launches_2024-0)[\[489\]](#refnote-SpaceX_Launches_2024) The satellites were deployed from the upper stage into the lower initial parking orbit with a perigee of 135km, less than half the targeted perigee.[&#8202;](#ref-back-501-0)[\[490\]](#refnote-501) After separation, the satellites were commanded to burn their ion thrusters. SpaceX modified the satellite software so the thrusters would produce as much thrust as possible.[&#8202;](#ref-back-502-0)[\[491\]](#refnote-502) Despite this, all of the satellites re-entered the atmosphere following the launch.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SpaceX-0)[\[492\]](#refnote-SpaceX) This launch was the first [Falcon 9 Block 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_Block_5 "Falcon 9 Block 5") or [Falcon 9 Full Thrust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_9_Full_Thrust "Falcon 9 Full Thrust") failure thereby ending the [Guinness World Record](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guinness_World_Records "Guinness World Records") of 325 successful Falcon 9 launches since the pre-flight anomaly of [AMOS-6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AMOS-6 "AMOS-6").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Clark-0)[\[493\]](#refnote-Clark)[&#8202;](#ref-back-guinnessworldrecords.com-0)[\[494\]](#refnote-guinnessworldrecords.com)

### Reuse of the rocket parts

Main articles: [SpaceX reusable launch system development program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_reusable_launch_system_development_program "SpaceX reusable launch system development program"), [List of Falcon 9 first-stage boosters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Falcon_9_first-stage_boosters "List of Falcon 9 first-stage boosters"), and [SpaceX fairing recovery program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_fairing_recovery_program "SpaceX fairing recovery program")

SpaceX has developed a program to reuse the first-stage booster and its fairings or Dragon capsules, setting multiple booster and fairing or Dragon reflight records respectively. Only second-stages are expendable, reducing launch costs significantly.

## Notes

01. [&#8202;](#refnote-1)[↑](#ref-back-1-0)[SpaceX CRS-7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_CRS-7 "SpaceX CRS-7") and [Starlink 9-3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Falcon_9_and_Falcon_Heavy_launches_(2024)#F9-354 "List of Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy launches (2024)")
02. [&#8202;](#refnote-2)[↑](#ref-back-2-0)The [AMOS-6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AMOS-6 "AMOS-6") spacecraft was destroyed in a static fire test before its planned launch; the mission is counted as a failure but not as a launch.
03. [&#8202;](#refnote-3)[↑](#ref-back-3-0)[SpaceX CRS-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_CRS-1 "SpaceX CRS-1")
04. [&#8202;](#refnote-13)[↑](#ref-back-13-0)There was also an on-pad explosion of an R-7 family rocket; sometimes it is counted as a launch, resulting in 64 launches.
05. [&#8202;](#refnote-18)[↑](#ref-back-18-0)Controlled descent; ocean touchdown control failed; no recovery
06. [&#8202;](#refnote-19)[↑](#ref-back-19-0)Passive reentry failed before parachute deployment
07. [&#8202;](#refnote-20)[↑](#ref-back-20-0)Controlled descent; soft vertical ocean touchdown; no recovery
08. [&#8202;](#refnote-note100)[↑](#ref-back-note100-0)While not reaching the goal of 100 launches in a calendar year, SpaceX completed 100 launches in 365 days between December 8, 2022, 22:27 UTC and December 8, 2023, 8:03 UTC.
09. [&#8202;](#refnote-booster)[1](#ref-back-booster-0) [2](#ref-back-booster-1) [3](#ref-back-booster-2) [4](#ref-back-booster-3)Falcon 9 first-stage boosters have a four-digit serial number. A hyphen followed by a number indicates the flight count. For example, B1021‑1 and B1021‑2 represent the first and second flights of booster [B1021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B1021 "B1021"). Boosters without a hyphen were expended on their first flight. Additionally, missions where boosters are making their first flight are shown with a mint-colored  background.

10. [&#8202;](#refnote-Dragon)[1](#ref-back-Dragon-0) [2](#ref-back-Dragon-1) [3](#ref-back-Dragon-2) [4](#ref-back-Dragon-3)Dragon spacecraft have a three-digit serial number. A hyphen followed by a number indicates the flight count. For example, C106‑1 and C106‑2 represent the first and second flights of [Dragon C106](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dragon_C106 "Dragon C106").
11. [&#8202;](#refnote-Noexactnessofpayload_mass)[1](#ref-back-Noexactnessofpayload_mass-0) [2](#ref-back-Noexactnessofpayload_mass-1) [3](#ref-back-Noexactnessofpayload_mass-2) [4](#ref-back-Noexactnessofpayload_mass-3) [5](#ref-back-Noexactnessofpayload_mass-4) [6](#ref-back-Noexactnessofpayload_mass-5) [7](#ref-back-Noexactnessofpayload_mass-6) [8](#ref-back-Noexactnessofpayload_mass-7) [9](#ref-back-Noexactnessofpayload_mass-8)Many _Transporter_ and _Bandwagon_ payloads are not public, or don't have a publicly revealed mass. SpaceX has not published a payload mass estimate for this mission.

Rumors started circulating on Monday that the satellite malfunctioned when it reached orbit, and both the _Wall Street Journal_ and _Bloomberg_ have reported that Zuma actually fell back to Earth and burned up in the planet's atmosphere. \[...\] SpaceX said that the Falcon 9 rocket, which carried Zuma to orbit, performed as it was supposed to. \[...\] "For clarity: after review of all data to date, Falcon 9 did everything correctly on Sunday night", \[Gwynne Shotwell\] said. "If we or others find otherwise based on further review, we will report it immediately. Information published that is contrary to this statement is categorically false". She added that the company cannot comment further due to the classified nature of the mission. \[...\] Of course, Northrop Grumman won't comment on the launch.
